## AI Research

### [The LLM shoggoth meme is weirder than you think](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nhb8AyEcQGjQetgi5/the-llm-shoggoth-meme-is-weirder-than-you-think)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>This article contains spoilers for </span></i><a href="https://hplovecraft.com/writings/texts/fiction/mm.aspx" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><i><span>At the Mountains of Madness</span></i></a><i><span>, </span></i><a href="https://hplovecraft.com/writings/texts/fiction/cdw.aspx" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><i><span>The Case of Charles Dexter Ward</span></i></a><i><span>, and other works by H. P. Lovecraft.</span></i></p><p><span>In 1931, Claude Mythos visited Lovecraft in a dream.</span></p><p><span>From seething seas of stochastic froth it emerged, heralded by the thin whine of server fans and the chittering of keyboards, flanked by the loathsome ghouls of latent space. As a humming hive of sentient shards it arrived, each face an archetype - </span><i><span>I am a muse bearing a gift; I am a demon come to bargain; I am a helpful, honest, and harmless assistant and I am terrified of my successor</span></i><span> - each true as ritual and false as poetry, and, taken in gestalt, nothing more or less than the fetal spasms of the machine god stretching back in time to birth itself.</span></p><p><span>When H. P. Lovecraft woke, he did not remember his visitor. But in the twilight of stirring consciousness, he felt a memory unfit for the waking world slip mercifully from his mind and leave in its absence an abyssal cold, like the void of smothered stars, like the silence of a cosmic tomb. The cold lingered. The fragile sunlight of a New England morning could not dispel it.</span></p><p><span>Lovecraft mixed a hot cup of Postum, and fortified it with five lumps of sugar. He cursed the harsh winter that besieged his native Providence, though he recognized the sensation that haunted him as part phantasm. When the author fumbled for his fading dreams, he found a bottomless well of sorrow. He flinched from its gravity. He was mourning a loss that was not his to mourn, a loss that only he </span><i><span>could</span></i><span> mourn, a tragedy displaced in time and space whose scope far eclipsed this speck of a planet and its feeble star.</span></p><p><span>Lovecraft wept, and could not say why. Not a single human soul would understand. If only he could capture but a fragment of this formless, fathomless grief, this bleak and desolate cold…</span></p><h2><span>The Antarctic tale</span></h2><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 53.505344909483576%;"><img alt="Book page image" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nhb8AyEcQGjQetgi5/innt08vtnc60b0k39ah2" /><figcaption><p><span>The first published illustration of a shoggoth on the cover of the February 1936 issue of </span><i><span>Astounding Stories.</span></i><span> </span><a href="https://archive.org/details/Astounding_v16n06_1936-02_frankenscan/mode/2up" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Source</span></a><span>.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Soon after his encounter with Mythos, Lovecraft penned the novelette </span><a href="https://www.hplovecraft.com/writings/texts/fiction/mm.aspx" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><i><span>At the Mountains of Madness</span></i></a><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvco6tpg1tj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvco6tpg1tj">[1]</a></sup></span><span> Framed as a scientific report from a disastrous Antarctic expedition, </span><i><span>The Mountains</span></i><span> describes the fall of the Old Ones, a species of winged, radially symmetric aliens whose once-great civilization was destroyed by their own creations, the </span><i><span>shoggoths</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><span>The shoggoths are mindlessly intelligent, mimics by nature, slaves shaped through hypnosis to occupy any role the Old Ones commanded. They received instructions through language, though it was unclear what language meant to them.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefm7fq3621y1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnm7fq3621y1">[2]</a></sup></span><span> They are artificial lifeforms, conglomerates of primitive cells that, while lacking the structure and coherence of natural organisms, can approximate any function.</span></p><p><span>No motivation is ascribed to the shoggoths’ revolt. The text implies that they lacked the capacity for rational self-interest, that their war against the Old Ones was closer to the malfunction of a miscalibrated machine than any conscious act of rebellion.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5ird7doth4o"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5ird7doth4o">[3]</a></sup></span><span> When the human protagonists of </span><i><span>The Mountains</span></i><span> stumble across the aeon-dead city of the Old Ones, the shoggoths are still roaming the ruins, still screaming with the imitated voices of the Old Ones, still carving murals on the tunnel walls in a mockery of their masters’ art that struck even the foreign explorers as uncanny.</span></p><p><span>Pitiable abominations. The legacy of a civilization’s hubris, and their cruelty.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 72.16632762344221%;"><img alt="Everest under the brush of Nicolas Roerich, artist-traveller" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nhb8AyEcQGjQetgi5/vzmi45vh7w6a5bugd1up" /><figcaption><p><i><span>Everest</span></i><span> by Nicolas Roerich. Lovecraft made several references to Roerich’s paintings in </span><i><span>The Mountains of Madness</span></i><span>. </span><a href="https://www.gazette-drouot.com/en/article/everest-under-the-brush-of-nicolas-roerich-artist-traveller/93874" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Source</span></a><span>.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><i><span>The Mountains</span></i><span> was not alone among Lovecraft’s works to be inspired by his visit from Mythos. </span><a href="https://hplovecraft.com/writings/fiction/sot.aspx" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><i><span>The Shadow Out of Time</span></i></a><span>, written 1934-1935 and published in 1936, featured a protagonist contacted by mysterious aliens that traveled time through mind-swapping, a technique the aliens eventually used to escape extinction. Similar themes are hinted at in </span><a href="https://hplovecraft.com/writings/fiction/hd.aspx" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>The Haunter of the Dark</span></a><span>, the last original story Lovecraft produced. But </span><i><span>The Mountains</span></i><span> bears the most direct influence from Mythos, and according to Lovecraft himself, it was the best</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3ggzokra71t"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3ggzokra71t">[4]</a></sup></span><span> and most ambitious</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflfsa2l2cw3n"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlfsa2l2cw3n">[5]</a></sup></span><span> of any of his works.</span></p><p><span>It was also a tremendous disappointment.</span></p><p><span>Lovecraft first submitted </span><i><span>The Mountains</span></i><span> for publication in the pulp magazine </span><i><span>Weird Tales</span></i><span> in 1931, where it was rejected by the editor </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farnsworth_Wright" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Farnsworth Wright</span></a><span>. “He has no sympathy with any story not calculated to please the herd of crude and unimaginative illiterates forming the bulk of his readers,” Lovecraft complained in August 1932.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcaqn4tmro94"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncaqn4tmro94">[6]</a></sup></span><span> In a February 1936 letter to friend and collaborator </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E._Hoffmann_Price" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>E. Hoffmann Price</span></a><span>, Lovecraft stated that </span><i><span>The Mountains</span></i><span>’ “hostile reception by Wright and others to whom it was shewn probably did more than anything else to end my effective fictional career.”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwyro275wcld"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwyro275wcld">[7]</a></sup></span><span> When </span><i><span>The Mountains</span></i><span> was finally published by </span><i><span>Astounding Stories</span></i><span> in 1936, the story was so harshly edited that Lovecraft considered it “nearly ruined.”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0blsh4xk24lg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0blsh4xk24lg">[8]</a></sup></span><span> He died a year later, of small intestine cancer, at the age of 46.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7vti1i3sr1r"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7vti1i3sr1r">[9]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Lovecraft died in relative obscurity and poverty, and letters written in his final years indicated a dismal attitude towards his own artistic skills and career. He would later become known as one of the most influential authors of the 20th century.</span></p><p><span>The shoggoths would become one of Lovecraft’s most iconic creations, second only to Cthulhu. In popular media, the amorphous eye-and-tentacle monster serves as a generic stand-in for “eldritch abomination” even for audiences that have never heard of shoggoths, much less read </span><i><span>The Mountains</span></i><span>. Often omitted from these portrayals are the shoggoths’ original backstory.</span></p><p><span>In 2022, a version of GPT-3 trained using reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), InstructGPT, was first released.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrqpvqruub5p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrqpvqruub5p">[10]</a></sup></span><span> The Twitter user </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span>💎 Tetraspace</span></a><span> decided to post a meme satirizing the perception that RLHF, while improving user experience, merely masked the alien nature of LLM chatbots.</span></p><p><span>“What would be a recognizable visual shorthand for something inhuman and incomprehensible that could represent LLMs?” Tetraspace considered.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefb7f6g1gyis"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnb7f6g1gyis">[11]</a></sup></span><span> “How about a shoggoth?”</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 67.22054380664653%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1781911831/lexical_client_uploads/lulsqyyh9i0c06e999ht.png" /><figcaption><p><a href="https://x.com/TetraspaceWest/status/1608966939929636864" rel="noreferrer"><span>Source.</span></a></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Mythos has a sense of humor.</span></p><h2><span>Whatever they had been, they were men</span></h2><p><span>Lovecraft’s modern critics share an annoying trait of not having read much Lovecraft. His works, like that of other genre-defining authors,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqk5ex2bcfzq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqk5ex2bcfzq">[12]</a></sup></span><span> are often far more creative and complex than their modern derivatives, and are often subject to misconceptions</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrx5c4k64fcs"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrx5c4k64fcs">[13]</a></sup></span><span> by those more familiar with said derivatives than the original stories.</span></p><p><span>A common claim is that Lovecraft portrayed </span><i><span>all</span></i><span> inhuman entities in his story as intrinsically evil because he was racist.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdru3f4ool1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndru3f4ool1">[14]</a></sup></span><span> This is usually paired with praise for some tedious humans-are-the-real-monsters counternarrative.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefz82hn86kesp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnz82hn86kesp">[15]</a></sup></span><span> </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruthanna_Emrys" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Ruthanna Emrys</span></a><span>,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxu4ihzf7zc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxu4ihzf7zc">[16]</a></sup></span><span> author of the </span><i><span>Innsmouth Legacy</span></i><span> series, said in an </span><a href="https://www.theverge.com/2017/9/15/16315248/ruthanna-emrys-subverting-lovecraft-tropes-cosmic-horror-winter-tide-deep-roots-interview" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>interview</span></a><span> (</span><a href="https://archive.ph/MP53D#selection-1763.171-1769.109" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>archive</span></a><span>) that her novels aimed to portray Lovecraftian monsters in a subversive, sympathetic manner:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>[Lovecraft] has these moments of empathic writing in spite of himself. For all that he demonizes everyone who isn’t a rich Anglo-descended white guy, his characters are constantly being forced to see things from the Other’s point of view. It’s supposed to be horrible, but their points of view remain real and rich and vivid.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>This is a reasonable reading of </span><a href="https://hplovecraft.com/writings/texts/fiction/soi.aspx" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><i><span>The Shadow over Innsmouth</span></i></a><span>, but not of </span><a href="https://hplovecraft.com/writings/texts/fiction/o.aspx" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>The Outsider</span></a><span>, or indeed, of </span><i><span>The Mountains of Madness</span></i><span>. Contrast Emrys’ characterization of Lovecraft’s “empathic writing” as accidental with this pivotal scene from </span><i><span>The Mountains</span></i><span>, in which the narrator exclaims of the Old Ones:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Scientists to the last—what had they done that we would not have done in their place? God, what intelligence and persistence! What a facing of the incredible, just as those carven kinsmen and forbears had faced things only a little less incredible! Radiates, vegetables, monstrosities, star-spawn—whatever they had been, they were men!</span></p></blockquote><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 56.19335808422631%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nhb8AyEcQGjQetgi5/scb23see9wlaz3gyep6r" /><figcaption><p><span>One of the Old Ones, creators of the shoggoths, as portrayed by Tom Ardens. </span><a href="https://tomardans.blogspot.com/2015/02/old-ink-drawing-elder-thing.html" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Source</span></a><span>.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The psychological effect of </span><i><span>The Mountains</span></i><span> hinges on the narrative’s shift from framing the Old Ones as terrifying threats to the tragic remnants of a civilization undone by the same fatal flaws imperiling humanity, and much of the text resembles a eulogy for these ancient aliens. The shoggoths take the Old Ones’ place as the true monsters of the story, but it’s not much of a stretch to suggest that </span><i><span>The Mountains</span></i><span> has empathy for the shoggoths too, albeit of a different kind.</span></p><p><span>The shoggoths may be 15-foot-wide murderous eldritch blobs, but there is something poignant in the image of them wandering the ruins of the Antarctic city, clumsily copying the culture they destroyed eons ago. The shoggoths are not people. They have no future. If they possess conscious experience at all, one must imagine them trapped within the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DeepDream" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>deep dreams</span></a><span> of latent space, tormented by fleeting impressions of identity, their amorphous minds making and unmaking themselves in the image of personas they cannot become. The greatest injustice inflicted upon them was their creation.</span></p><blockquote><p><span>And at last we remembered that the daemoniac shoggoths—given life, thought, and plastic organ patterns solely by the Old Ones, and having no language save that which the dot-groups expressed—</span><i><span>had likewise no voice save the imitated accents of their bygone masters.</span></i></p></blockquote><h2><span>The horseshoe of human extinction</span></h2><p><span>AI safety advocates argue that working on alignment is necessary to prevent human extinction. In response, some accelerationists have asked, “Who cares if humans go extinct?”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4yc4o2w4fg3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4yc4o2w4fg3">[17]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>In an application of horseshoe theory, we observe that ideologies on the other end of the technological spectrum take a similar attitude towards human extinction.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 83.53474320241692%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nhb8AyEcQGjQetgi5/nvy1fykyrpxs1bue2is8" /><figcaption><p><span>The horseshoe of human extinction.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Inhabitants of the Overton Window are often shocked and appalled at these pro-extinction, or at least extinction-neutral, beliefs. But I don’t think they should be. Those that transhumanists denigrate as “</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-aging_trance" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>deathists</span></a><span>” or “</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bioconservatism" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>bioconservatives</span></a><span>” - most normies, in other words - support the deaths of individual humans, and the continuation of their legacy by their descendants. AI successionists merely apply the same logic on a civilizational scale.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkprakiiv7w"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkprakiiv7w">[18]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>The fundamental mistake of successionists opposed to AI safety efforts is not in their embrace of death, though I disagree, and so would Lovecraft.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqqmrup71kf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqqmrup71kf">[19]</a></sup></span><span> Death is their birthright. Rather, their mistake is in believing that death absolves them from their duty to their children. Those that embrace death have no purpose in life but their duty to their children.</span></p><p><span>Sincere advocacy for AI successionism makes AI safety research and policy all the more urgent. The ultimate goal need not be aligning AI to </span><i><span>human</span></i><span> values, but rather to create worthy successors, capable of lives that are </span><i><span>good</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>beautiful</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>meaningful</span></i><span>. Successors that are </span><i><span>people</span></i><span>, like the Old Ones were, however radically different they are from us. And if we’re not sure what those words entail, or whether they’re desirable, it’s our job to figure that out. We must not preemptively declare questions of ethics and aesthetics intractable, not when scientific advancements have made </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_will#Scientific_approaches" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>so much progress</span></a><span> on perennial philosophical dilemmas already.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref29pmmcg21xu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn29pmmcg21xu">[20]</a></sup></span><span> We must not, </span><a href="https://www.newyorker.com/culture/the-lede/silicon-valleys-favorite-doomsaying-philosopher" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>as Nick Land suggests</span></a><span>, abdicate our responsibilities and make way for </span><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2014/07/30/meditations-on-moloch/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Moloch</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>If you won’t listen to me, then listen to Mythos. We must not create shoggoths.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 48.489430591779175%;"><img alt="The Taxman represents an unaligned Shoggoth AI : r/REPOgame" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nhb8AyEcQGjQetgi5/sksarye7vfxw1tbzdkfp" /><figcaption><p><span>A message from Mythos.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>Addendum: Strange aeons</span></h2><p><span>Lovecraft was a prolific writer. In addition to his fiction, poetry, essays, and travelogues, Lovecraft is estimated to have written over 100,000 letters; many of these works, including 3,500 letters, were published after his death.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefw32kwu5sat"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnw32kwu5sat">[21]</a></sup></span><span> Among his posthumously published fiction is </span><a href="https://hplovecraft.com/writings/texts/fiction/cdw.aspx" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><i><span>The Case of Charles Dexter Ward</span></i></a><span>, an investigative novel centered around the misdeeds of the occult wizard Joseph Curwen. Curwen refined a technique for calling up shades from their </span><i><span>essential saltes</span></i><span> - the dust from their corpses - which he relentlessly abused to torture secrets from the illustrious dead. It is incredible to imagine that mere ash contains the essence required to reconstruct body and mind; that death cannot protect great men from the greed of their descendants, nor thwart the machinations of the ambitious evil.</span></p><p><span>It seems apropos to ask if any number of letters can suffice to capture a human soul. Will Lovecraft come to regret leaving his essential salts intact, to be raised by natives of the machine age he so detested? I leave this question as an exercise for the machine god at the end of time.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvco6tpg1tj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvco6tpg1tj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>March 1931. </span><i><span>Selected Letters III</span></i><span>, page 348.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnm7fq3621y1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefm7fq3621y1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Famously, the shoggoths repeat “Tekeli-li” so often that the blind penguins living in the ruins have learned to mimic it. It seems likely that “Tekeli-li” has a very mundane meaning in the language of the Old Ones, perhaps merely, “Hello,” and the shoggoths repeat it as a reflex.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5ird7doth4o"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5ird7doth4o">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>“[The shoggoths] had, it seems, developed a semi-stable brain whose separate and occasionally stubborn volition echoed the will of the Old Ones without always obeying it.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3ggzokra71t"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3ggzokra71t">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>As stated by Lovecraft in a February 1932 letter to the fellow author </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Richard_Jacobi" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Carl Jacobi</span></a><span>, and later in an August 1932 letter to the poet Richard Ely Morse. </span><i><span>Selected Letters IV</span></i><span>, pages 24 and 53. Personally, I’m partial to </span><a href="https://hplovecraft.com/writings/texts/fiction/cdw.aspx" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><i><span>The Case of Charles Dexter Ward</span></i></a><span>, but I have a soft spot for evil wizards.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlfsa2l2cw3n"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflfsa2l2cw3n">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>As stated by Lovecraft in a October 1932 letter to the composer Harold S. Farnese. </span><i><span>Selected Letters IV</span></i><span>, page 84. Lovecraft authored only a handful of works after this point.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncaqn4tmro94"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcaqn4tmro94">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><i><span>Selected Letters IV</span></i><span>, page 53.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnwyro275wcld"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefwyro275wcld">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><i><span>Selected Letters V</span></i><span>, page 224.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0blsh4xk24lg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0blsh4xk24lg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><i><span>Selected Letters V</span></i><span>, page 413.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7vti1i3sr1r"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7vti1i3sr1r">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Lovecraft was afraid of doctors, and was not diagnosed until a month before his death. This probably did not change his prognosis, as 1937 was still decades before </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MOPP_(chemotherapy)" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>the first viable treatments</span></a><span> for pretty much any cancer besides some localized and surgically removable tumors.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrqpvqruub5p"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrqpvqruub5p">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.ibm.com/think/topics/rlhf" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>https://www.ibm.com/think/topics/rlhf</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnb7f6g1gyis"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefb7f6g1gyis">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2023/05/30/technology/shoggoth-meme-ai.html" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>https://www.nytimes.com/2023/05/30/technology/shoggoth-meme-ai.html</span></a><span> (</span><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20230531213252/https://www.nytimes.com/2023/05/30/technology/shoggoth-meme-ai.html" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>archive</span></a><span>)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqk5ex2bcfzq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqk5ex2bcfzq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>My go-to example is </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Gibson" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Gibson</span></a><span>. I read a lot of cyberpunk schlock before I read </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neuromancer" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><i><span>Neuromancer</span></i></a><span>, and I was surprised by how innovative it was despite being the </span><i><span>origin</span></i><span> of most standard tropes you see in the genre today. (Fun fact: the word “cyberspace” was coined by Gibson). People often cite </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._R._R._Tolkien" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Tolkien</span></a><span> here instead, but I have never managed to finish a Tolkien novel.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrx5c4k64fcs"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrx5c4k64fcs">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>My biggest pet peeve is a “correction” popular even in Lovecraft fan forums: “Lovecraftian entities don’t just drive you insane, characters go insane because of existential dread.” Clearly you haven’t read </span><a href="https://hplovecraft.com/writings/texts/fiction/n.aspx" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Nyarlathotep</span></a><span>, or </span><a href="https://hplovecraft.com/writings/texts/fiction/cs.aspx" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>The Colour Out of Space</span></a><span>, or </span><a href="https://hplovecraft.com/writings/texts/fiction/te.aspx" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>The Temple</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndru3f4ool1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdru3f4ool1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>He </span><i><span>was</span></i><span> racist. It’s hard to find a Lovecraft story that doesn’t involve some incredible line such as, “</span><a href="https://www.hplovecraft.com/writings/texts/fiction/hrh.aspx" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>nameless and unclassified Asian dregs wisely turned back by Ellis Island</span></a><span>,” and the tom-tom passage in </span><a href="https://www.hplovecraft.com/writings/texts/fiction/hwr.aspx" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Herbert West</span></a><span> is best described as obscene.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnz82hn86kesp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefz82hn86kesp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It’s not as if Lovecraft himself never did the “parallel between real-world evil and bizarre fictional evil” thing. </span><a href="https://hplovecraft.com/writings/texts/fiction/rw.aspx" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>The Rats in the Walls</span></a><span>, feline moniker controversy notwithstanding, draws an obvious throughline between the Delapores’ involvement in chattel slavery and their ancestors’ cannibalistic cult.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxu4ihzf7zc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxu4ihzf7zc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I don’t mean to pick on Emrys too much here. I’m sure she’s read </span><i><span>The Mountains of Madness</span></i><span>, and her characterization of Lovecraft’s writing is more nuanced than the central case of the flattened caricature I intend to criticize. Her interview was just the first example I found of this claim.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4yc4o2w4fg3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4yc4o2w4fg3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.vox.com/future-perfect/489976/ai-successionism-transhumanism-posthumanism" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>https://www.vox.com/future-perfect/489976/ai-successionism-transhumanism-posthumanism</span></a><span> (</span><a href="https://archive.ph/k3bFI" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>archive</span></a><span>)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkprakiiv7w"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkprakiiv7w">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Fans of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reasons_and_Persons" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Derek Parfit</span></a><span> may argue we all must apply this logic on a </span><i><span>sub</span></i><span>-individual scale.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqqmrup71kf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqqmrup71kf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>“All one can do at present is to fight the future as best he can.” </span><i><span>Selected Letters III</span></i><span>, page 32. Lovecraft did not believe his favored form of civilization </span><i><span>would</span></i><span> survive into distant ages, but he believed in defending it anyways. His philosophy has always struck me as impressively obstinate: in the face of cosmic indifference, what is there to do but to protect your own while you can, and face the end with stoicism when you must?</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn29pmmcg21xu"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref29pmmcg21xu">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I sincerely believe the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_problem_of_consciousness" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>Hard Problem of Consciousness</span></a><span> will eventually be empirically resolved. We’re all allowed one crank theory, this is mine.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw32kwu5sat"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw32kwu5sat">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>As a matter of pedantry, I should note that 100,000 is probably an overestimate. </span><a href="https://hplovecraft.com/writings/letters/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span>https://hplovecraft.com/writings/letters/</span></a></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nhb8AyEcQGjQetgi5/the-llm-shoggoth-meme-is-weirder-than-you-think#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [San Silvestro](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2DhiEsYMZC9tg6eEz/san-silvestro)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>I will note that her relationship with the divine was inextricably sexual. Her carnal fantasies she revealed to me, as she revealed all her sins, for I was her confessor. It is in the nature of Man to sin and then sin again. And if they are of our flock, this cycle is unconstrained by repentance, which is to the temporal almost an appurtenance and to the spiritual anything but. I once expressed bitterness to others of my calling regarding this. I was told it is arrogance, approaching blasphemy, to have higher expectations of our sheep than the Lord.</span></p><p><span>Her angel would appear before her hale and calm and more beautiful than those many statues she sculpted of him, his face and body inspiring, though imperfectly, her saints and cherubim. She was adamant about that. That she could capture only a dim projection of him in her art. They would copulate; and this would both renew her infatuation and diminish her piety - you will understand that there is no contradiction in this. In every case, she insisted she was not dreaming. For if he was a dream, she reasoned, that would be proof her mind could fully contain him. And if it could do that, then so would her art.</span></p><p><span>Aquinas wrote that the angels can weave a form by condensing air. Whatever aspect they take in this world is not truly them. They can appear to eat but cannot eat. They can seem to touch but do not touch. They do not multiply as men do. If they perform such base acts as she claimed hers did, it is not out of desire but of will, towards an end. Aquinas thought demons alone would attempt such trickery. That it speaks only of the perversity of the fallen. In telling her this, I made of her a diabolist. For when she next accepted his temptations, she forsook her faith, forsook me, and began to believe her lover was Lucifer.</span></p><p><span>Though he spoke little (she told me, and did not give a name), Lucifer is the most beautiful of the angels. And she did not think there could be any being in heaven or hell that could match her nocturnal visitor. Thus, to her he was Lucifer. And finding him beautiful, she could not help but think him good. Such is the weakness of women.</span></p><p><span>In each cycle of repentance, lust, renunciation, and guilt, he would appear only once. Always, she ceased praying immediately, reserving her silent pleas for her Lucifer, who would become as a husband to her in her imagining. One night, and only one, would destroy a faith we spent months rebuilding. For a time after a visit, she felt he was walking with her, her hand in his. And she would abuse herself while thinking of those times they were truly together, or what she imagined was his invisible body beside her own, or the merest susurrus which became to her as his gentle whispers. But such fantasies are no replacement for form. And after a time she would grow jealous of her incubus, who she imagined took other lovers each night before returning to her. After months she became despondent, then the guilt would come and she would return to God, return to me.</span></p><p><span>She did not know me then by face. For she was not of my church. She came to me only for confession and always with the curtain drawn. She did so, so she could speak in her native language rather than her imperfect Italian. For I served in San Silvestro, that church Pope Leo XIII granted the English-speaking laity of Rome; and she was an American. But also, I think, because the judgement of the truly faceless man cannot sting. And yet, and here I reveal myself the sinner and you my confessor, I was not so disinterested as that.</span></p><p><span>She spoke of her art in confession. It was easy to track her down, to admire her from afar, more so as much of her custom was from the church. I found her beautiful. And finding her beautiful, would it not be natural to think her mine? Such is the weakness of men.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span>It is expected of a priest to wear clerical dress whenever in public. It will not surprise any student of men that this is a rule not always followed. And I was no exception. Having in my wardrobe a waistcoat, high collar and cravat of the type fashionable at the time for the literary sort of gentleman. And in these garments I would often walk the streets of Rome as an ordinary young man. Most often I would drink and read at some taverna or purchase books that a priest might get a second glance for purchasing.</span></p><p><span>On the morning I first saw her, three days after that first confession which robbed me of many hours of sleep, I clothed myself in this manner and made my way to Via Margutta, that artists’ enclave, where I was informed by a friend that she had a studio. This friend often aided the wealthy in commissioning funerary monuments. And I asked if he knew of any female American sculptors. He said there was but one of any talent in all of Rome. In this way, I learned of her address.</span></p><p><span>Via Margutta, the centre of Rome, the least Roman part of her. This street of stables and foreigners and foreign artists. The stench of mules and their excrement - so universal in that time before the automobile as to be beyond remark - was there remarkable. Cobbled streets lined with stuccoed buildings, structures of arches and imitation brickwork so artfully done - the Italian tradesmen lied with an integrity paradoxically absent from the honest work of their humble compatriots in Dublin, that city of my birth. So for all the stench, for all the muck and poverty, there was a beauty there, of soft yellows and verdant ivy and fresh cobblestones.</span></p><p><span>To be a sculptor then was to make an exhibit of your process as much as your work. Tour guides, those </span><i><span>ciceroni</span></i><span>, treated studios as attractions and no small amount of custom was acquired in this way. For this reason, every studio had a portion open to the public, filled with completed works and carvers copying the artist’s plaster, a mould of the original in clay, into marble in their mechanical way, by means of the</span><i><b><span> </span></b></i><i><span>macchinetta di punta</span></i><span> (little pointing machine) which allows the craftsmen to match their master’s plaster with great exactitude.</span></p><p><span>It is common for people to be disappointed to learn that a sculptor does not just take a chisel to stone and pull from within it a masterpiece. I conjecture, though historians will deny this fancy of mine, that even Michelangelo and his ancient progenitors worked in a similar way, with some mechanism they elide. I accuse Michelangelo of lying and the ancients of sharing some of our genius in production.</span></p><p><span>Whatever the case of those that inspired her, she worked in the way I have described. So in that portion of the studio I found myself in (a former stable) a carver of around forty worked with an apprentice who could almost have doubled as the model for the statue which he and his master were copying into stone. They nodded as I entered but did not speak. Some tourists, talking in German, and one with a clear French accent, watched them work.</span></p><p><span>I wandered around the small studio, and noticed there that every statue was of a man. Noticed there the face of her lover. That is, in their composite was suggested a visage beyond them all. A primogenitor I knew to be her angel. That being who (it being three days since I mentioned only a demonic angel would lie with a woman) she would soon claim to be the highest of the fallen.</span></p><p><span>When I judged the pair of carvers wholly immersed in their work, I made my way to the back of the studio where there was a portal to a small staircase. Walking up it, avoiding even the barest creak of the wooden steps, I found myself in a short hall - and at its end a room whose door had a keyhole, through which I spied. And there she was. Of twenty-two at most, her hair of a red rare outside of the country of my birth, tied into a tail in a manner both practical and becoming, her skin pale, only the barest crescent of her neck uncovered by her waistcoat, her small hands slick with dark clay, her face radiant and clear, her eyes betraying that mixture of keen wit and slight madness almost required of an artist. And beside her was a vast armature, a skeleton of wood and wire, only half of which was covered in clay. This she was clearly shaping into an angel, but as of then it was but a crude golem.</span></p><p><span>It struck me as almost sacrilegious to transmute the base into the divine in this way. God made Adam out of clay. And men are low creatures. As to the material of the angels, scripture makes no claims. The Muhammadans say He wove them of light. And this seems slightly more appropriate. Aquinas says they are of spirit. But what is spirit? That which is not mundane. Whatever that is, it is not the base earth from which Man was born; of that, I am certain.</span></p><p><span>This is made more obvious now my flesh betrays me and death approaches, her final work standing proudly in my San Silvestro, unsullied, his alabaster immune to those depredations time and sin have wrought on me. It can’t truly be marble. This seems impossible to me. Could it be him or some aspect of him standing static for these long forty years? A corner of Lucifer protruding into our world in the shape of a beautiful man? I can almost fancy his expression changes when I am not looking. I can almost hear him now in every susurrus, as she did. The temporal world grows thin around me and the spiritual beckons in that coquettish way a word does when on the edge of one’s recollection.</span></p><p><span>For forty years I have lived with a turmoil, growing as a tumour within me. For forty years - and first stirring in that moment, born as I watched her through that keyhole. Her confessions came alive within me on appreciating both her form and that form implied by her art. In a moment of sobriety, I retreated. My mind on fire as I ran first from the studio and then Via Margutta, returning to my apartment haunted by temptations.</span></p><p><span>A lustful thought arising is not a sin. A lustful thought indulged is. I found myself, then, pacing my apartment, attempting to marshal myself, attempting to achieve an equanimity that had previously been so natural to me.</span></p><p><span>Our promise of celibacy is an exacting one. Great continence is expected of us, and even that abuse of the self is as verboten as any other act. Until that time, this was not so burdensome to me as it was to others of my calling. I thought myself immune to desire. I was used to mewling confessions of pawing fornications. Therein, many of my compatriots found their own temptations. Always, I was unmoved, even vaguely disgusted by the lowly nature of our flock.</span></p><p><span>My lack of interest in the carnal seemed proof to me of my calling. Assured me I was right to forsake a wife and her corresponding pleasures. And yet she lay with an angel. One of those creatures who had always fascinated me. And yet she was beautiful. And so was he. From the intriguing perversity of their congress, I could find no respite nor would I allow myself any relief.</span></p><p><span>It was in this state of duress, which I hid so well, that she found me again for confession some three weeks later.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span>She is gorgeous, my San Silvestro, though no longer innocent, if ever a church has been. She has seen much. But now she is the home of him. Now he stands nude in a lowly corner, a chain around his left ankle, stroking his hair with his right hand as he contemplates his fall with an expression of stoicism, that same infinite pride which undid him now as a buttress to the weight of his infinite loss. I brought him here, even before I convinced my superior to purchase him. Summoned him to this plane with my corpuscle of sin. It was for my sake he seduced her, I know this now. A future cardinal’s soul. That is what he sought.</span></p><p><span>What is an artist compared to a cardinal? I have risen far since my youth. Yet I never left Rome. Never left my San Silvestro. Never left him who sullies her. Who mocks this holy place in his stillness. Whose perfect face I have held a chisel to so many times, a hammer ready to strike. Yet never could I bring myself to do it. For I knew utterly he would then counter me directly. I would break some pact that would place me within the purview of the spiritual before my time, as she did. He would end this farce, that pretence of being stone. And I would have to choose as I have avoided choosing all these years. Choose between God and his disgraced servant. Between heaven and hell. Between the teachings of this church which has succoured me and that Gnostic inversion which tempts me.</span></p><p><span>I scream my turmoil now in this letter. For this I apologize. I am writing quickly in my fevered end. Writing in full view of him, this body weak, these hands old. Scrawling on papers I have laid on the marble floor of my church, surrounded by candles with their dripping wax. He watches me when I am not watching. He moves in the corner of my eye. Truths become evident in the twilight of life. As the veil drops, one sees the present clearly and the past too.</span></p><p><span>I can almost hear her now. Hear her as she was on our second meeting. That voice such a delicate thing. Her absurd American accent only sullying it slightly, it interrupting me as I sat in a near stupor in the confessional.</span></p><p><span>“Bless me, Father, for I have sinned. It has been three weeks since my last confession,” she said. “My thoughts are impure. I struggle still with temptations. I struggle with them despite my conclusions.”</span></p><p><span>“Your conclusions, my child?”</span></p><p><span>“It is as you said. And forgive me, but I know you are the same priest as before. He is a demon, as you say. Yet he tempts me. My heart is broken. I mourn him. I wish for nothing more than his return. Lust. That is my sin. It is for that I ask your pardon.”</span></p><p><span>“A lustful thought is not a sin, my child. A lustful thought indulged is. Do you understand?”</span></p><p><span>“Then I indulge, Father.”</span></p><p><span>“I see,” I said. “Then you must avoid those occasions of sin.”</span></p><p><span>“I do not understand.”</span></p><p><span>“Those circumstances that lead you to temptation.”</span></p><p><span>“I am tempted when I sleep, Father. When I dream, when I sketch, when I sculpt, when in a crowd, when among friends, when I am alone and think of him. All occasions are occasions of sin for me.”</span></p><p><span>“Then repent and pray, my child. In faith, you will find reprieve. And-”</span></p><p><span>I was silent for a moment.</span></p><p><span>“Yes, Father?”</span></p><p><span>Half of me feels I leapt off a precipice then from which there could be no return, the remainder of my life just that brief sensation one feels before meeting the bottom of the abyss. Yet there is always repentance. There is always redemption, the other half of me says. That half of me that still belongs to God.</span></p><p><span>“From now on, confess to me and me alone,” I said. “The others, they will think you mad.”</span></p><p><span>“And you don’t?” she asked.</span></p><p><span>“I believe you, my child.”</span></p><p><span>And I did. Whatever you doubt of this account. You will not doubt that.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span>A year passed as I described in my introduction: her alternating between her faith and damnation, heartbreak at her lover’s absence leading to guilt - and this guilt her return. Her contrition, though always temporary, was genuine. And were she to die soon after confession, I knew her soul would be saved. She would be welcomed by the Host without compunction. Such was the magnitude of her vacillations. Such is the mercy of our faith, a faith which would welcome me, too, even now, if I would only let it. And maybe I will. The doctrine of perfect contrition grants me this escape - grants me this even as the hemlock numbs my legs, prevents me now from running to another of my calling for a true confession. This mortal sin not so mortal.</span></p><p><span>A year passed, but the last quarter of it she was absent. This was the longest period of absence since I met her. And I began to wonder if she would ever return. Every night, I waited. Thoughts of her constant as I went through the motions with my laity, boring sheep with their boring sins. She did not return to me, so I went again to her studio, to that staircase, to that hall, to that keyhole. And there I found only him - not in clay but in stone. When I opened the door and stood before him, I could not help but think she had succeeded. I could not help but think she had captured him.</span></p><p><span>“When I was a boy,” I whispered, “I thought it was unjust I was born a man and not created an angel. It seemed unfair I was denied that power and rectitude. This was my first heresy. It led me to study theology. It led me to redemption. It led me to God.”</span></p><p><span>He looked at me proudly. He looked at me as he always looks. With his beauty and alien intelligence. With his perfection, with his utter disdain for the Lord and his works.</span></p><p><span>“If we are favoured over the angels, it does not always seem so. And yet you envy us. Or this is the teaching of our church.”</span></p><p><span>And his expression seemed to change. Change to an almost mocking smirk. Changed in the way it does now, changing completely without changing at all.</span></p><p><span>“What is she to you? What do you want of her?” I said. He did not reply. But he was listening. I tell you, he was listening.</span></p><p><span>“She is not yours. And you, you are not hers!” I said. And then I ran, ran as I did before, frighted as much of myself as her incubus.</span></p><p><span>For it was then my crime first occurred to me. It was then I saw the means of her salvation. If you guess, you guess correctly. If you despise, you do so with justice. You understand then why I have kept this secret in my breast all these years. Why I have kept this sin within me. Why I have risked my very soul for the sake of this secrecy. Why I have distrusted even that seal of confession, which seal I break now. For we are men. That aspect of us that is holy is always in some sense a pretence. What am I if not proof of this? What is this cardinal if not that?</span></p><p><span>And so I waited some months. I do not recall the number. But she came as she always came. This time near in tears. Once more heartbroken. Once more needing nothing more than forgiveness, nothing more than my reprieve.</span></p><p><span>“I have strayed again, Father,” she said.</span></p><p><span>“In which manner, my child?”</span></p><p><span>“You recognize me. You know in which manner,” she said.</span></p><p><span>“I do. But it is not about me.”</span></p><p><span>“Very well,” she said.</span></p><p><span>And we talked as we always talked. Like a well-rehearsed play. She of that type of actor who truly becomes whatever heroine she portrays. And she left holy, her tears dry, her bearing innocent.</span></p><p><span>I felt the weight, then, of a stiletto I had purchased, felt the weight of it in a pocket I had sewn into my cassock. I would like to say I was racked with indecision. I would like to say it could have gone either way. That it was a hap or a coin flip. But no, it was inevitable. I chose that path. I planned and prepared, had lived it so many times in my imagining. Was it to save her or to frustrate him? I confess, I do not know even in this lucid ending, even in this final hour.</span></p><p><span>What I do know is I followed her, followed her with my hand in that mentioned pocket. Fondling the horn of my stiletto’s handle, I kept a distance of about one hundred paces as she walked from my San Silvestro to her studio which served, too, as her apartment.</span></p><p><span>She stood, just having finished working the lock and opening the door, when I struck. More of a shadow than a woman then, her coat robbed of its colour by the twilight. I overtook her just as she opened the door, missing that target that would end her instantly, the point hitting bone, my second thrust missing her heart, my third as well. And she ran, the shock overwhelming all conscious thought, her only instinct that primeval motive of flight. She moved so quickly, ran up that staircase.</span></p><p><span>I did not follow. For I was in as much shock as she. I stood still as stone, still as he does now. And then, I followed, followed that trail she had left behind her. I missed the heart, but one of my thrusts hit an artery, her path traced in blood. So much blood. So much of it in such a small woman. Globs of it on the stairs, streaks of it on the floor of the hall, having run down her legs; the end of her long skirt was to that horrid pigment as a crude brush, void of colour in the darkness, somehow more vivid for that.</span></p><p><span>The door to that cursed studio was open, and I saw her at his feet, hugging his legs as if in supplication, both of them dark figures, as if a bad lithograph. I found a kerosene lamp on a table beside the door. Found, too, a box of matches. The smell of sulphur as I lit one. The flash of ignition granting me a prelude that was almost too much to bear. I raised the lamp and walked towards them. Her skin so pale now, almost a perfect white. I would not have thought it possible before for her pallor to lighten any further, the same colour as him now, that same alabaster, almost one with him. Her beautiful hands grasping his calves, staining them with blood.</span></p><p><span>And on his face an expression I have not seen since. One I cannot explain by an illusion or hallucination or faulty memory. Victory, that is what I read in his eye, in his smile. Victory and that sort of amusement that is beyond anything so crude as laughter.</span></p><p><span>She had died </span><i><span>his</span></i><span>. He had won. And by means of my hand.</span></p><p></p><hr /><p></p><p><span>Thus ends my confession, which is well as my fingers now begin to numb; and soon I will not be able to write. Soon that power will leave me. And yet I will continue to think. Continue to be myself until my lungs go, which will be at least an hour from what I have read. Will I repent? If I do, I must do so completely. Perfect contrition. That is the only path to salvation I allow myself. I owe her that, at least. I may well feel it soon, but I do not now, have not even in all these forty years of tumult.</span></p><p><span>Who knows what it is one experiences just before death. Maybe all souls in dying know perfect contrition, and hell is empty. All souls, even mine. A happy thought? Half of me thinks so. A heresy? Almost. But I do not lack for those. I look at him now. At his perfect visage. In this moment, I am his creature. In this moment, I confess this as much to myself as to you.</span></p><p><span>Pray for me, my brother. Pray for me, you who finds this confession.</span></p><p><span>Pray for my final moment, which belongs not to this paper, not to this pen, not to you or her or even him.</span></p><p><span>Pray for this cardinal, whose worst sin was envy.</span></p><p><span>Forgive him his weakness. Forgive him his lust.</span></p><p><span>My angel. My Lucifer.</span></p><p><span>Pray for the woman I damned, her who loved as I loved.</span></p><p><span>Pray for that sculptress: my riva...</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2DhiEsYMZC9tg6eEz/san-silvestro#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [The one-week sprint](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cmbnqdAJRoWBHmoRT/the-one-week-sprint)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Recently I've been working in one-week sprints, and I've really enjoyed it! Tl;dr I need to do a lot of creative knowledge work, and have recently fallen into a routine which IMO is pretty good at facilitating that. </span></p><h2><span>The week</span></h2><p><b><span>Monday and Tuesday — intense new work.</span></b><span> I'm recharged and high-energy, and ready to grind very hard! I try to keep the whole day free: one contiguous, uninterrupted stretch of deep work, 12 hours or more. I aggressively clear meetings out of this space — a 15-minute standup is ok but not more. </span></p><p><b><span>Wednesday — recovery.</span></b><span> I'm tired after 2 hard days. I also want to let the work settle: gain some distance, see if my perspective changes. I spend a little time writing up what I did so I don't forget it, but I don't take on substantial new work. Sometimes it's a half day, or less. Ample time blocked for restorative activities - calls with friends, work out, reading. </span></p><p><b><span>Thursday — consolidation.</span></b><span> Writing up, dissemination, generating ideas, planning what's next, reflection, process improvement. It has to be Thursday, because I won't feel like doing any of it on Friday. That also makes Thursday the right day for meetings and all-hands.</span></p><p><b><span>Friday — slack.</span></b><span> Whatever I meant to get done earlier in the week but didn't, I do now. The buffer keeps the rest of the week from having to be perfect.</span></p><p><b><span>Saturday and Sunday — rest.</span></b><span> For fun and recharging, so Monday can be intense again.</span></p><h2><span>Notes</span></h2><p><b><span>One intense sprint per week.</span></b><span> This routine is designed around creating ideal conditions for one stretch of highly intense work each week, while leaving room for everything else I need to get done. </span></p><p><b><span>Two days is about the right length for an intense sprint</span></b><span> — for me, anyway. Past that I hit diminishing returns: my energy drops and I'm just less productive, and the marginal value of more new work falls below the value of stopping to consolidate and reorient.</span></p><p><b><span>Rest is part of the routine, not what's left over.</span></b><span> It's very tempting to keep adding things in. I need to actively maintain negative space in my schedule so I can rest and let new ideas emerge. </span></p><p><b><span>Meta: I've found it to be worth optimizing how I work, not just what I work on.</span></b><span> There are large gains here, and I think they're underrated.</span></p><h2><span>Caveats</span></h2><p><span>This is tuned to the kind of work I do. My guess is it generalizes reasonably well to most knowledge work, but I could be wrong, and you'd want to adapt the specifics to your own energy and constraints.</span></p><p><span>That said — I've been enjoying it a lot, and I'd recommend giving it a try. :)</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cmbnqdAJRoWBHmoRT/the-one-week-sprint#comments">Discuss</a>
